Let me understand the task. This is a noninterference proof for a locally nameless STLC with security annotations. This is based on the classic "Security via logical relations" style proof (e.g., from the "Proving Noninterference" literature, like the Tait-style logical relation for SLam calculus, as in Azevedo de Amorim et al. or the Software Foundations "Security" chapters).

Let me look at the card.md too.

Let me plan the logical relation. The standard approach for this SLam-like language (from the "Noninterference" chapter of the TLC / "Security" notes) is:

Define a binary logical relation on terms at a type T relative to a security level. Typically:

```
Fixpoint interp (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop := ...
```

The key structure: since `transparent T_result` and `ground T_result` and the secret's indirect_reader can't flow to result's indirect_reader, we need to show that the erased results are equal.

Let me think about the specific structure of this language. This is likely derived from the "logical relation for noninterference" in the style of the paper "A Semantic Model for Noninterference" (maybe from the "Security" chapter of the "Formal Reasoning About Programs" or a classic exercise).

Actually this looks like it's derived from a known benchmark suite: "100__security-stlc__noninterference__if__hard" — perhaps from the "Rocq benchmarks" of the POPL? The naming suggests benchmarks from "LLM benchmarks for Rocq" perhaps. Regardless, we need to construct the proof ourselves.

Let me think about the semantics.

Types have security annotations. Terms have protection annotations. The typing rules have the "protect" semantics: application result type is `ty_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) T2`, and the step rule for application wraps an `open body v` in `tm_protect kappa.(indirect_reader)`. When a protect is applied to a value, it upgrades the value's security annotation (via protect_value). Then the "security levels" on values govern information flow.

The noninterference theorem: If `t1`, `t2` have the same secret type `T_secret`, then placing them in a context C producing a `T_result` which is ground and transparent, and where secret indirect_reader cannot flow to result indirect_reader, then all evaluations give equal erased results.

The standard proof uses a logical relation indexed by type, relating pairs of terms that are "indistinguishable" at that type. Given the security levels, the relation likely is:

For a type T with security kappa, define `obs T` or similar: the relation `V_T(v1, v2)` on values:
- For `Ty_Unit kappa`: if kappa.(reader) = Low (or `flows_to ...`?), then erase_security v1 = erase_security v2. Actually with `public` = Low/Low and `secret` = High/High.

Hmm, let's think about the semantics carefully. The security record has `reader` and `indirect_reader`. The `reader` level determines who can read the value: High means secret (only high readers may observe it). The `indirect_reader` determines the level of the reader who observed the value indirectly (via control flow).

Standard SLam calculus (from "A Semantic Model for Noninterference" / the security chapter): security level with two components, and `protect_security kappa l` joins both with l. Value typing with security on the type. The logical relation typically:

`V[T](v1, v2)`:
- if `security_of T = public` (i.e., reader = Low?): erase_security v1 = erase_security v2
- otherwise: True (any two values are related at a secret type)

Hmm, but `ground` and `transparent` are needed for the target theorem: ground T_result means T_result is a unit/sum/prod (no arrows), transparent means all security annotations along the way are ≤ security_of T_result... Actually wait: transparent_at is a weird "variance": `security_le (security_of T) kappa` and recursive on components with the same kappa. So `transparent T = transparent_at (security_of T) T`. For a product `Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa`, transparent means security_of T1 ≤ kappa, security_of T2 ≤ kappa (both readers and indirect readers), similarly recursively. For arrows, transparent_at recurses into both T1 and T2 with same kappa (contravariant, but they don't care).

Hmm, so `transparent` basically says all security annotations of all components are below the top-level one. Since security_le requires both reader flow and indirect reader flow. With labels Low/High, flows_to l1 l2 is False only when l1=High, l2=Low. So security_le kappa1 kappa2 means reader1 ≤ reader2 and indirect1 ≤ indirect2 in the order Low < High.

`ground T` means no arrows.

So for a ground transparent T_result, all the security annotations inside are ≤ top-level. Since `~ flows_to secret's indirect_reader result's indirect_reader`: if secret indirect_reader = High and result indirect_reader = Low, then... Hmm, the only forbidden combination is High → Low. So the assumption says: NOT (secret indirect_reader = High AND result indirect_reader = Low). Which means either secret indirect_reader = Low, or result indirect_reader = High.

Hmm, how does the proof use this? The context C has type: has_type (update empty x T_secret) (plug C (tm_fvar x)) T_result. We need to relate plug C t1 and plug C t2. The claim then: any context that doesn't leak via indirect_reader gives equal results. The relation at secret type must be universal for well-typed closed terms, and the context lemma must show the context maps universal-relatedness at the secret type to erased equality at T_result.

Let me think about the design ourselves. We'll need to make the proof work; we can derive needed lemmas as long as the target theorem is provable.

Let's first understand the type system and semantics fully, then find a sound logical relation and prove fundamental theorem.

Key definitions:
- labels Low, High. flows_to l1 l2 = False iff l1=High, l2=Low; else True. So flows_to is the order Low ≤ High (Low flows to High, and reflexivity). join is max: Low∨Low=Low else High.
- security = {reader, indirect_reader}; wf_security kappa := flows_to kappa.indirect_reader kappa.reader, i.e., indirect_reader ≤ reader (Low ≤ High allowed; High ≤ Low not).
- security_le k1 k2 := reader1 ≤ reader2 ∧ indirect1 ≤ indirect2.
- public = Low/Low; secret = High/High.
- protect_security kappa l joins both levels with l: so protection raises both to at least l.

Types: Ty_Unit kappa, Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa, Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa, Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa.
security_of T = the top-level annotation kappa.
ty_protect l T = protect the top-level annotation only.
wf_ty: recursively all annotations wf_security (indirect_reader ≤ reader).

Terms: bvar, fvar, unit security, abs ty body security, app t1 t2 label, pair t1 t2 security, fst t label, snd t label, inl t security, inr t security, case t0 body1 body2 label, protect label t, if c yes no label.

Step relation:
- AppAbs: value (abs T body kappa), value v, flows_to kappa.reader r → app (abs..) v r → protect kappa.indirect_reader (open body v).
   Interesting: the label r on the app is only checked by the type system (T_App requires flows_to kappa.reader r); at runtime, r doesn't appear in result. The result is protected by kappa.indirect_reader.
- FstPair: value pair v1 v2 kappa, flows_to kappa.reader r → fst (pair v1 v2 kappa) r → protect kappa.indirect_reader v1.
- Case: case (inl v kappa) body1 body2 r → protect kappa.indirect_reader (open body1 v) given flows_to kappa.reader r.
- ProtectValue: protect l v → protect_value v l (for value v).
- If: if (inl v k) yes no r → protect k.indirect_reader yes, given flows_to k.reader r.
- If test: condition must have type Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public) (Ty_Unit public) k, i.e., sum of public units with annotation k. Interesting: the two branches of the condition type are both `Ty_Unit public`, so the payload is public; the sum's annotation k determines the indirect reader level.

Noninterference intuition: secret value of type T_secret not correlated with result unless the result type's indirect_reader can flow from secret's indirect_reader... Hmm, the assumption `~ flows_to secret.indirect_reader result.indirect_reader`. Note that "flows_to High Low = False", so the assumption is essentially: secret.indirect_reader = Low OR result.indirect_reader = High. That seems backwards from what I'd expect: noninterference usually requires secret level (High) not flow to result level (Low), i.e., `~ flows_to secret_reader result_reader` or `~ flows_to secret_level result_level`. Here the assumption is `~ flows_to secret.indirect_reader result.indirect_reader`, i.e., we cannot have secret's indirect_reader=High and result's indirect_reader=Low. Hmm.

Wait, but maybe I have it backwards: maybe the theorem is about *indirect* flows: the context uses the secret value; the relevant leaking path is the *indirect_reader* of the secret becoming high and the result being observable... Hmm no.

Hold on. Perhaps the intended relation is: A value of type T can be observed by a reader at level `security_of T`.(reader). If reader=Low, then public. The indirect_reader component tracks the context level under which it was produced. The noninterference theorem's side condition should ideally be `~ flows_to secret.reader result.reader` to prevent direct flows. But the given theorem uses indirect_reader. Hmm, but note wf_security kappa: indirect_reader ≤ reader. So if result.reader = Low then result.indirect_reader = Low. And if secret.reader = High... then secret.indirect_reader could be Low or High. The condition ~ flows_to secret.indirect result.indirect: if result.indirect = Low and secret.indirect = High, forbidden.

Hmm, consider T_secret = secret unit (High, High), T_result = public unit (Low, Low): forbidden (High doesn't flow to Low). So this direction is *forbidden*, i.e., the assumption says secret's indirect_reader must not be above result's indirect_reader. If secret.indirect is High and result.indirect is Low, then the proof cannot establish noninterference.

I think the security levels should be interpreted as: "reader" is the level of the *principal allowed to read* the value; "indirect_reader" is the level of the principal that can learn about the value through *indirect* means (context). A secret has High reader; a low value has Low.

Hmm, perhaps the intended relation at type T compares two values at the level of `indirect_reader`?? Let me think about the condition needed for the context lemma. Let me carefully think about the semantics and design a suitable logical relation.

First, let me get a sense of the whole system: this is the SLam calculus with security annotations. Let me recall the literature: "SLam" is from "A semantic model for noninterference" maybe by "Amtoft"? Actually the name SLam calculus appears in "Noninterference via logical relations" by "Azevedo de Amorim"? Or maybe it's the calculus from the paper "A Programming Language for Information Flow Security" ... Hmm.

I recall the "SLam" calculus from the paper "Noninterference for a simply typed lambda calculus with references" by Honda, Yoshida? The "security types" with protect. Hmm. More likely, this benchmark is generated from a base proof in some repository, and features (if-then-else) are added.

Key question: what logical relation makes the theorem provable?

Let me think semantically. Consider a value v of type T. The security annotation kappa = security_of T has reader level and indirect_reader level. Intuition:
- If kappa.reader = Low, the value is public and can be observed (its erase_security should be equal across two runs).
- If kappa.reader = High, the value is secret: no observation required.
- The indirect_reader component: when a value is used in a "context" (e.g. as a condition for if), the resulting branch's result gets protected by the condition's indirect_reader (join). So the indirect_reader propagates: if a secret condition influences which branch is taken, the branch result is tainted with the secret's indirect_reader level. Then the result type's security annotation is raised accordingly, which may make it High, which then makes it unobservable.

The theorem condition `~ flows_to secret.indirect_reader result.indirect_reader` seems weird; let's compute with `wf_ty` assumptions. Are types wf? `has_type empty t1 T_secret` and wf constraints: we can derive wf_ty T_secret? Since `has_type` has wf premises everywhere, likely we can prove `has_type Gamma t T -> wf_ty T`. Hmm, T_Sub: subtype T U with wf premises... S_Trans doesn't have wf premises but can combine. Actually S_Unit requires wf_security both. S_Sum etc. require wf_security for the top annotations, but subtype doesn't necessarily preserve wf_ty recursively? Let's see S_Sum requires subtype T1 U1 and T2 U2 plus wf top annotations; subtype of components may themselves be derived without wf? S_Trans: subtype T U and U V; no wf requirements, so can compose arbitrary. But every leaf S_* rule requires wf_security top-level. So by induction, subtype T U implies wf_ty T? For S_Trans, use IH on first. For S_Sum, need wf_ty (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa1): components wf from IH of subtype T1 U1, T2 U2, plus wf_security kappa1. Yes! So subtype T U → wf_ty T (and wf_ty U? For U, S_Trans second gives IH; S_Sum gives IH of subtypes plus wf_security kappa2. Yes both). And has_type → wf_ty T by induction probably. For T_Var, wf_ty T premise. T_App: wf_ty (ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T2) needs wf_ty T2 (from IH has_type Gamma t2... no wait, T_App's result type uses T2 from the arrow type; the premises give has_type Gamma t1 (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) so IH gives wf_ty of the arrow = wf_ty T1 ∧ wf_ty T2 ∧ wf_security kappa. Then ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T2 preserves wf_ty? Hmm, ty_protect only changes the top annotation to protect_security kappa' l. Is protect_security kappa l wf when kappa wf? wf_security (protect_security kappa l) means join(indirect kappa, l) ≤ join(reader kappa, l). If l=Low: join is identity? join x Low = x for x∈{Low,High}? join Low Low = Low; join High Low = High (second case `_,_ => High`). Yes join x Low = x. So protect with Low keeps both unchanged: protect_security kappa Low = kappa. If l=High: join x High = High for both, so protected = High/High which is wf. So yes, protect_security preserves wf_security. Good.

So we can expect wf_ty T_secret and wf_ty T_result. Ground T_result and transparent T_result given.

Now let's think about the logical relation design.

Standard approach for a language with "reader" security levels:

Define two relations:
1. Value relation `V_T(v1, v2)` (or perhaps `V_T(v1, v2)` meaning indistinguishable to a Low observer).
2. Expression/term relation `E_T(t1, t2)` := for all v1 v2, t1 ⇓ v1 → t2 ⇓ v2 → V_T(v1, v2).

For noninterference at low result type, we need V_T at public type to imply erase equality for ground transparent T.

Since T_secret can be secret (High), and t1, t2 are both well typed at T_secret without being related by erasure, the relation at a secret type should be universal. That suggests:

`V_{Ty_Unit kappa}(v1, v2)` := if kappa.reader = Low? Hmm, but if kappa.reader = High, all values related; if Low, erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.

But wait: what about indirect_reader? The theorem's side condition mentions indirect_reader, and the context_has_type typing uses the secret at T_secret, and the context's typing rules produce protections. Consider a context that uses the hole as a condition of an if: the condition type must be `Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public) (Ty_Unit public) k`, so the hole's type T_secret must be a subtype of this sum type. The sum's annotation k can be arbitrary. The if rule requires `flows_to k.reader r` and returns `ty_protect k.indirect_reader T`. The runtime: if inl/inr of the condition value, step to protect k.indirect_reader (branch). So the branch gets protected by the condition's indirect_reader.

Hmm, so consider secret = Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public)(Ty_Unit public) secret? A value tm_inl (tm_unit public) secret? Condition's annotation would be secret → k.reader = High, k.indirect_reader = High → result protected High. Then result type has annotation with indirect_reader High. Then the final result type's indirect_reader... the theorem assumes ~ flows_to secret.indirect_reader result.indirect_reader where secret.indirect = High, so result.indirect must be High (to make flows_to High High = True → negation False... wait, ~ flows_to High High = ~True = False). Hold on! If secret.indirect_reader = High, then ~ flows_to High result.indirect is ~True = False when result.indirect = High, and ~False = True when result.indirect = Low.

So if T_secret is `secret` (indirect High), the theorem requires result.indirect_reader = Low. That means the result type's indirect_reader must be Low, i.e., no secret indirect flow into the result.

Wait, I think I had it backwards. The assumption `~ flows_to secret.indirect result.indirect` with the actual `flows_to` (forbidding High→Low) says "secret.indirect does NOT flow to result.indirect"... Hmm, nomenclature: flows_to l1 l2 is the relation "l1 flows to l2" = allowed. High→Low is not allowed. So the assumption says: the secret's indirect_reader is not allowed to flow to result's indirect_reader. In a lattice, "not allowed to flow" means the secret information cannot reach the result's indirect level. So if secret.indirect = High and result.indirect = Low, then flow would be disallowed (True assumption says it's NOT the case) — wait no: the assumption ~ flows_to High Low = ~False = True, so that case is *allowed* by the assumption. Hmm! That means the assumption permits the dangerous case High→Low? That seems inverted.

Let me recheck flows_to definition: 
```
match l1, l2 with
| High, Low => False
| _, _ => True
```
So flows_to High Low = False. "Secret flows to public" is False — that's the standard: information at High cannot flow to Low. So `flows_to l1 l2` = True means "information at l1 is permitted to flow to l2" = l1 ≤ l2 (Low ≤ High).

The theorem assumes `~ flows_to secret.indirect result.indirect`: information at secret.indirect is NOT permitted to flow to result.indirect. Hmm, that's the opposite of what noninterference needs! Noninterference needs: secret information does not actually flow to the public result. Usually you'd assume `~ flows_to secret_level result_level`, i.e., secret cannot be observed at result. Hmm, but here it's indirect_reader to indirect_reader.

Standard approach: prove a general "fundamental theorem" for the logical relation on *open* terms: define a context of related substitutions, etc. Alternatively, prove by induction on C the lemma:

The typical way: prove the fundamental theorem for the logical relation as a *Kripke* relation on open terms with a notion of "related environments": For all Gamma, t, T, has_type Gamma t T → for all (substitutions) ... Standard Tait-style: define the logical relation on open terms via a notion of closing substitution: `Gamma ⊨ t1 ≈ t2 : T` iff for all related substitutions σ1 ≈ σ2 : Gamma, t1[σ1] ≈ t2[σ2] : T (in E). Since substitution in locally nameless here is for free variables (tm_fvar) and the language has no free-variable opening for evaluation; `has_type` uses open body (fvar x) and substitution is on fvar. The locally nameless part (bvar) is handled via open on evaluation. Note that `open` and `subst` are different: `open body v` substitutes for the outermost bound variable (bvar 0); `subst x u t` substitutes free variable x. In this language, terms in typing derivations are "opened" bodies with fvar x, and evaluation opens bodies with values.

Definition rel_env (Gamma : context) (sigma1 sigma2 : list (atom * tm)) : Prop :=

Both sides have u inserted under the same binders (since subst_env doesn't change the binder structure and open_rec on both sides inserts at the same depth). So the equality holds regardless! Capture affects the meaning but both sides are syntactically equal. Good.

Let me estimate the size: This is a research-level proof, but the language is small and the relation can be designed to make it manageable. Let's design carefully to simplify:

Idea: Make the value relation V *ignore* the structure of "secret" values entirely, and for public structures use erasure equality. Specifically:

V_T(v1,v2) := 
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit kappa => (kappa.ind = High) \/ (erase_security v1 = erase_security v2)  [maybe True if High]
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa => 
       if kappa.ind = High then True
       else (exists w1 w2,... both inl and V T1) \/ (both inr and V T2)
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa =>
       if kappa.ind = High then True
       else exists w1 w1' w2 w2', v1 = pair w1 w2 _ /\ v2 = pair w1' w2' _ /\ V T1 w1 w1' /\ V T2 w2 w2'
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
       if kappa.ind = High then True   (* ? *)
       else forall r u1 u2, V T1 u1 u2 -> E (ty_protect kappa.ind T2) (app v1 u1 r) (app v2 u2 r)
  end.

Wait, but can we make the arrow relation True when kappa.ind=High? The result type of an application is ty_protect kappa.ind T2 which then has ind High, so the relation at the result type is universal (if the relation's top-level guard is ind=High). And indeed, all we need is E at that universal type, which should be trivially true. So V at arrow with ind=High can be True. That's convenient. Similarly unit/prod/sum with ind=High: universal. This makes the relation "observational at indirect level": if the type's ind is High, no constraints.

Now, what about the reader component? It's used in typing premises flows_to kappa.reader r. Where do we need it in the proof? In the T_App compatibility case, we know has_type Gamma t1 (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) and flows_to kappa.reader r. The actual value of t1 is an abs with annotation kappa' and subtype relation to kappa. We need the beta step to happen: ST_AppAbs requires flows_to kappa'.reader r. From subtyping, kappa'.reader ≤ kappa.reader, so flows_to kappa'.reader r follows by transitivity. And the runtime result is protect_value/... protected by kappa'.ind, while the declared result type is ty_protect kappa.ind T2. We need relation at ty_protect kappa.ind T2. Since V at ind High is True, the only problematic case is kappa.ind = Low, in which case the declared type ty_protect kappa.ind T2 = T2 (since join x Low = x) and the runtime protection uses kappa'.ind ≤ kappa.ind = Low, so kappa'.ind = Low too, and ty_protect kappa'.ind T2' = T2'. Then relation at T2 from body relation at T2', with subtyping T2' <: T2. Hmm, we need monotonicity of the relation under subtyping.

(a) For values that are functions (self-relatedness in the fundamental theorem);

Since the two functions may be different, we need the full application relation. So we need the decomposition lemma for multi on app. This requires general lemmas about multi/step inversion. Let me think about how to prove such decomposition lemmas elegantly. Option: prove "step deterministic"? Is step deterministic? ST_ProtectValue vs ST_Protect could both apply? Standard approach: define evaluation in "big-step" style? We could define a big-step evaluation relation `eval : tm -> tm -> Prop` and prove it equivalent to multi+value. Then the fundamental theorem could use big-step inversion rules which are cleaner (the standard big-step rules for CBV): 
- eval unit unit
- eval (abs) (abs) [if value]
- eval (app t1 t2 r) v: eval t1 (abs T body κ) -> eval t2 arg -> eval (protect κ.ind (open body arg)) v
- etc.
Then the compatibility proof uses big-step inversion on the goal's hypotheses. But the target theorem's conclusion uses `evaluates` (multi-based). We'd prove `evaluates t v <-> eval t v`. Proving equivalence requires the small-step decomposition lemmas anyway. Hmm, but maybe easier: prove `eval t v -> multi t v ∧ value v` and `multi t v -> value v -> eval t v` by induction. The latter needs decomposition of multi into single steps; manageable: induction on multi: multi_refl case → eval t t requires value t → the big-step rule for values (all values evaluate to themselves). multi_step: t1 --> t2 -->* t3; by IH eval t2 t3; need eval t1 t3 from a single step. So we need "step preserves big-step eval": step t1 t2 -> eval t2 v -> eval t1 v. This is a "step inversion/closure" lemma, proved by cases on step and inversion on eval. Might be manageable but still requires inversion on eval for each construct.

Let me think about the cleanest formalization. Prove:
```
Lemma multi_app_inv : forall t1 t2 r v,
  multi (tm_app t1 t2 r) v -> value v ->
  exists f a, multi t1 f /\ value f /\ multi t2 a /\ value a /\ multi (tm_app f a r) v.
```
Proof: induction on multi? The derivation of multi (app t1 t2 r) v is a chain. Induction on the chain: 
- multi_refl: v = app t1 t2 r, and value v contradicts (app is not a value) — need lemma `value_app : value (tm_app ...) -> False`, easy by inversion.
- multi_step (app t1 t2 r) t2' v: step (app t1 t2 r) t2'; then multi t2' v; IH on the tail gives decomposition of t2'. Then case on the first step:
  - ST_App1: t1 --> t1', t2' = app t1' t2 r. IH gives f,a with multi t1' f etc. Then multi t1 f via step, and app f a r ⇓ v. Good.
  - ST_App2: value t1, t2 --> t2', t2' = app t1 t2' r. IH gives f,a with multi t1 f, value f, multi t2' a, value a, multi (app f a r) v. Then multi t2 a via step. Good.
  - ST_AppAbs: t1 = abs T body κ, value, t2 value, flows_to κ.reader r, t2' = protect κ.ind (open body t2). IH gives f,a with multi (abs...) f, value f, multi (abs...) a?... wait, the IH applies to the app in t2': t2' = protect κ.ind (open body t2), which is not an app! Oops. The IH is stated for app-shaped terms: multi (app t1 t2 r) v. After the beta step, the term is a protect, not an app. So induction on the multi derivation fails at the beta case because the shape changes.

Hmm. So the decomposition lemma must be proved differently. Standard approach: prove a "multi step decomposition" by induction on the multi derivation with a generalized statement. Alternatively, note: to decompose app evaluation, we can use the fact that multi is the reflexive transitive closure of step, and prove using a "last step" analysis: a value v can't step; the last step into v must be... Maybe simpler: prove the decomposition via a distinguished "evaluation relation" as an inductive big-step relation and prove it equivalent to multi. This is the standard route: define `eval` with big-step rules; prove small-step-to-big-step and big-step-to-small-step. The latter is easy by induction on eval (each big-step rule corresponds to small steps and we can concatenate multi). The former (multi -> eval) needs the "step preserves eval" lemma: if t1 --> t2 and eval t2 v then eval t1 v. Prove by induction on the step derivation, inverting eval t2 v. This is manageable: cases on step; each case inverts one eval rule.

Let me write out big-step rules for this CBV language:

```
Inductive eval : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
| E_Unit : forall kappa, eval (tm_unit kappa) (tm_unit kappa)
| E_Abs : forall T body kappa, value (tm_abs T body kappa) -> eval (tm_abs T body kappa) (tm_abs T body kappa)
| E_Pair : forall v1 v2 kappa, eval v1 v1? ...
```
Hmm, for values, the eval rule should be "v ⇓ v" for all values: `E_Value : forall v, value v -> eval v v`. Then compound rules:

- E_Pair: eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> eval (pair t1 t2 κ) (pair v1 v2 κ) — note the value of pair needs values.

So congruence holds only for contexts that don't go under binders: C_Abs, C_CaseLeft, C_CaseRight, and C_Abs... Let's see the step rules:

- eval (tm_abs T body κ) (tm_abs T body κ) if value (tm_abs T body κ)
- eval (tm_pair t1 t2 κ) (tm_pair v1 v2 κ) if eval t1 v1, eval t2 v2. But then for a pair *value* tm_pair v1 v2 κ, E_Pair fires with eval v1 v1? We'd need eval v1 v1 which requires eval of values... Since values can be nested pairs, E_Pair recursively handles: eval (pair v1 v2 κ) (pair v1 v2 κ) using IH eval v1 v1 and eval v2 v2, which need value rules for v1, v2. So we need `E_Value`-like rules anyway for base values. If we have E_Unit, E_Abs, E_Inl, E_Inr (via recursive rules), it's fine: E_Inl: eval t v -> eval (inl t κ) (inl v κ). For t = v a value: recursively eval v v via its own form's rule. Every value's form has a rule: unit, abs (with value premise), pair, inl, inr. For abs: E_Abs with `value (tm_abs T body κ)` premise. So yes, define eval with one rule per syntactic form, including:

```
Inductive eval : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
| E_Unit : forall k, eval (tm_unit k) (tm_unit k)
| E_Abs : forall T body k, value (tm_abs T body k) -> eval (tm_abs T body k) (tm_abs T body k)
| E_Pair : forall t1 t2 k v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> eval (tm_pair t1 t2 k) (tm_pair v1 v2 k)
| E_Fst : forall t r v1 v2 k w, eval t (tm_pair v1 v2 k) -> value v1 -> value v2 -> flows_to k.reader r -> eval (tm_protect k.indirect_reader v1) w -> eval (tm_fst t r) w
| E_Snd : ...
| E_Inl : forall t k v, eval t v -> eval (tm_inl t k) (tm_inl v k)
| E_Inr : ...
| E_Case : forall t b1 b2 r v k w, eval t (tm_inl v k) -> value v -> flows_to k.reader r -> eval (tm_protect k.indirect_reader (open b1 v)) w -> eval (tm_case t b1 b2 r) w
| E_CaseR : similar with inr
| E_Protect : forall t l v w, eval t v -> eval (tm_protect_value v l) w -> eval (tm_protect l t) w
| E_App : forall t1 t2 T body k r arg w, eval t1 (tm_abs T body k) -> eval t2 arg -> value (tm_abs T body k) -> value arg -> flows_to k.reader r -> eval (tm_protect k.indirect_reader (open body arg)) w -> eval (tm_app t1 t2 r) w
| E_Fst etc.
| E_IfL/E_IfR.
```

```
Fixpoint subst_env (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=

  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_unit k => tm_unit k
  | ...
```
This is simultaneous substitution. We require σ to map "fresh" variables (not bound) appropriately; since bound vars are bvars, no shadowing issues. Good.

Let's define:
```
Definition top_security (v : tm) : security :=
  match v with
  | tm_unit k => k
  | tm_abs _ _ k => k
  | tm_pair _ _ k => k
  | tm_inl _ k => k
  | tm_inr _ k => k
  | _ => public
  end.

Definition annotation_ok (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  security_le (top_security v) (security_of T).
```
Hmm, is `security_le (top v) (security_of T)` the right invariant? Note that for a value at type T with a *sum* type: the value inl w κ' — the annotation κ' is the *sum's* annotation (from tm_inl's κ), so top_security gives κ' and security_of T = κ; require κ' ≤ κ. Good.

Then:
```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k =>
      annotation_ok T v1 /\ annotation_ok T v2 /\
      (match k.ind with High => True | Low => erase_security v1 = erase_security v2 end)
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      annotation_ok T v1 /\ annotation_ok T v2 /\
      (match k.ind with
       | High => True
       | Low => (exists w1 w2, v1 = tm_inl w1 k /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k /\ V T1 w1 w2)
             \/ (exists w1 w2, v1 = tm_inr w1 k /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k /\ V T2 w1 w2)
       end)
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 k => ... exists w1 w1' w2 w2', v1 = tm_pair w1 w2 k /\ v2 = tm_pair w1' w2' k /\ V T1 w1 w1' /\ V T2 w2 w2'
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      annotation_ok T v1 /\ annotation_ok T v2 /\
      (match k.ind with
       | High => True
       | Low => forall r u1 u2, E T1 u1 u2 -> E (ty_protect k.ind T2) (tm_app v1 u1 r) (tm_app v2 u2 r)
       end)
  end
with E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2.
```
Note: in the sum/prod matching, should the annotations in the value pattern equal k? The value inl w κ' with declared type Ty_Sum T1 T2 k: κ' ≤ k but not necessarily equal. So the pattern should not force κ' = k. Use `exists w1 w2, v1 = tm_inl w1 κ1 ...` with arbitrary annotation; the annotation clause handles the relation. So pattern match on constructor and extract the annotation variables:
`(exists (w1 : tm) (k1 : security), v1 = tm_inl w1 k1) /\ ...` with V T1. Let's write:
`(exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ V T1 w1 w2) \/ (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\ V T2 w1 w2)`.
Similarly pairs.

```

Let's think about what "indirect" means for noninterference with a context. The context might use the secret in a control-flow decision (if/case), causing the *result* to depend on the secret indirectly. The result type's annotation gets protected by the condition's indirect_reader (which is ≥ secret's indirect_reader if the secret flows into the condition). So if the result type's indirect_reader is Low, then the secret's indirect_reader must be Low too (otherwise the protection would have raised it). Hence the noninterference precondition is `~ flows_to secret.ind result.ind` — i.e., it's NOT the case that secret.ind ≤ result.ind: the secret indirect level is not low enough to be observed at the result indirect level. With labels: secret.ind=High > result.ind=Low. Exactly: if the secret's indirect level were ≤ result's, then the result could indirectly leak it. So the observer at result.ind = Low must not learn about secret.ind = High.

This relation is indexed by κ and monotone in κ: if flows_to κ1 κ2? Hmm: V κ T should be *weaker* for smaller κ? ```
Fixpoint V (kappa : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k => ... erase equality when flows_to k.ind kappa ...
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k => ...
  | Ty_Prod ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      if flows_to k.ind kappa then
        (forall r u1 u2, E kappa T1 u1 u2 -> E kappa (ty_protect k.ind T2) (app v1 u1 r) (app v2 u2 r))
      else True
  end
```

Let's look at the actual definition:

```
Fixpoint transparent_at (kappa : security) (T : ty) : Prop :=
  security_le (security_of T) kappa /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      transparent_at kappa T1 /\ transparent_at kappa T2
  end.
```

V arrow: ann + (κ.ind=High → True) + (κ.ind=Low → forall r u1 u2, lc u1 -> lc u2 -> E T1 u1 u2 -> E T2 (app closure1 u1 r) (app closure2 u2 r)).
So we intro r u1 u2 Hlc1 Hlc2 HE. Then to prove E T2 of the apps: intro w1 w2 Heval1 Heval2; need V T2 w1 w2.
Invert Heval1 (eval (app (tm_abs T1 (subst σ1 body) κ) u1 r) w1): E_App gives:
  eval (tm_abs T1 (subst σ1 body) κ) (tm_abs T body k) — inversion on eval of an abs yields T=T1, body=subst σ1 body, k=κ, and value; 
  eval u1 arg1, value arg1, flows_to κ.reader r, eval (protect κ.ind (open (subst σ1 body) arg1)) w1.
Now, I want to apply the body IH with env extended by x ↦ arg1 (the evaluated argument value), not u1. Because the commutation lemma gives subst_env σ' (open body (fvar x)) = open (subst_env σ' body) u where σ' x = u; if σ' x = arg1 then we get open (subst_env σ body) arg1 provided x ∉ fv body. But the app's body is subst σ1 body and the runtime opens with arg1: open (subst σ1 body) arg1. So we need σ' with x ↦ arg1. And the env relation requires E T1 arg1 arg2 and lc arg1/arg2.
We have HE : E T1 u1 u2. Apply to eval u1 arg1 and eval u2 arg2 → V T1 arg1 arg2. Then E T1 arg1 arg2 from V + values (arg1, arg2 are values from E_App) via eval uniqueness. lc arg1/arg2 from eval_lc.
Then IH at Hx with env σ1' = extend x arg1 σ1 (and x also maybe the env entry for other vars), σ2' = extend x arg2 σ2, gives E T2 (subst_env σ1' (open body (fvar x))) (subst_env σ2' (...)).
Commutation: subst_env σ1' (open body (fvar x)) = open (subst_env σ1' body) arg1 (by subst_env_open_fvar + σ1' x = arg1). And subst_env σ1' body = subst_env σ1 body since x ∉ fv body. So = open (subst_env σ1 body) arg1. 
So E T2 (open (subst σ1 body) arg1) (open (subst σ2 body) arg2).
Now the eval hypotheses have protect κ.ind of these. Since κ.ind=Low in this branch, E_protect_low gives E T2 (protect Low (open ...)) (...). Apply to Heval1, Heval2 → V T2 w1 w2. Done.

Great. Note: we used eval inversion for the app. We also need `eval_lc`: eval t v -> lc_at 0 v. Let's prove by induction on eval: E_Unit: lc; E_Abs: value abs gives lc_at 0 (abs) (value means locally_closed (abs) = lc_at 0); E_Pair: value (pair v1 v2 k) needs value v1,v2 which follow from eval v1... IH gives lc of v1,v2? Actually E_Pair: eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> eval (pair ...) (pair v1 v2 k). Need lc_at 0 (pair v1 v2 k) = lc v1 ∧ lc v2; from IHs. But do we know value v1,v2? Not needed for lc. Good.
E_Fst: result is some w with eval (protect k.ind v1) w; IH gives lc w.
Etc. But careful: the eval constructors' premises give the IH for sub-evals. Fine.

Good, do induction on the value derivation generalizing w.

Now, the E_App inversion: since eval is an inductive with constructors having distinct head constructors? Let's list eval constructors and their conclusion heads:

Each head distinct! Great, so `inversion H` on `eval (tm_app t1 t2 r) w` produces exactly the E_App case (others are discriminated by constructor). Excellent. Inverting eval (tm_abs ...) v gives E_Abs with v = tm_abs same. Good. Inversion should generate equations; we'll manage.

Hmm, one thing: with constructors like E_Pair having premises eval t1 v1, inversion on eval (tm_pair ...) w will give v1,v2 and the equation w = tm_pair v1 v2 k. Fine.

Now, the `E` definition and `V` being a Fixpoint: Let me write V carefully so that Coq accepts the fixpoint and computations simplify. We'll need the label match to reduce. Define:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k =>
      ann T v1 /\ ann T v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => erase_security v1 = erase_security v2
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      ann T v1 /\ ann T v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
              v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ V T1 w1 w2)
          \/ (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
              v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\ V T2 w1 w2)
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 k => similar with pairs
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      ann T v1 /\ ann T v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          forall (r : label) (u1 u2 : tm),
            locally_closed u1 -> locally_closed u2 -> E T1 u1 u2 ->
            (forall (x1 x2 : tm),
                eval (tm_app v1 u1 r) x1 -> eval (tm_app v2 u2 r) x2 ->
                V (ty_protect k.(indirect_reader) T2) x1 x2)
      | High => True
      end
  end
with E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop := ...
```
Wait, E defined in the same Fixpoint block: E T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2. Mutual fixpoint: V calls E T1 and E T2? In the arrow case I inlined the E of the result type as a forall over evals, but I used E T1 for arguments. Using E T1 (same block) with T1 a subterm of T — mutual fixpoint structural check: In the mutual block, V's principal argument is T; E's is T. V calls E T1 where T1 is a subterm of T: allowed if the guard checker sees E's argument as structurally smaller than V's argument. I believe Coq's mutual fixpoint allows calls to another function in the block with a structurally smaller argument. Let's test. If not, we can inline E everywhere (write the argument relation as a forall-eval too). Let's plan to inline to be safe? That makes V's definition verbose but fine. Actually, defining E in the mutual block is standard (e.g., logical relations). Let's try; if it fails, inline.

Hmm, but there's a catch: my arrow clause currently quantifies `E T1 u1 u2 ->` and then the result as a forall over evals. If I define E in the same block, the result could just be `E (ty_protect k.ind T2) (app v1 u1 r) (app v2 u2 r)`, which is cleaner and definitionally equal. Let's try the mutual definition:
```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop := ...
with E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2.
```
In V's arrow case, call E T1 (T1 subterm of T) and E (ty_protect k.ind T2) — uh oh! ty_protect returns a type of the *same size* as T2, which is a subterm of T, structurally smaller than T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k. Coq's guard checker should accept `E (ty_protect k.ind T2)`: the argument is a function application, not a syntactic subterm — Coq's guard checker typically does NOT accept arbitrary function applications in recursive calls; it requires the argument to be a variable or a subterm. `ty_protect k.ind T2` is an application, which would be rejected as "not a subterm". Hmm. Also recursively, the size is the same as T2 but Coq can't see it.

Workaround: avoid calling E on ty_protect types inside V. Since ty_protect only changes the top annotation, we can define a helper that reconstructs the type with the same structure? Coq guard checker still won't see it as smaller. Alternative: make the result relation conditional on k.ind:
- If k.ind = Low: ty_protect Low T2 = T2, so call E T2 (subterm) directly.
- If k.ind = High: V at ty_protect High T2 is True, so no call needed.
So in the arrow case, write:
```
match k.ind with
| Low => forall r u1 u2, lc u1 -> lc u2 -> E T1 u1 u2 -> E T2 (app v1 u1 r) (app v2 u2 r)
| High => True
end
```
And then prove a lemma that this is equivalent to V at ty_protect k.ind T2 in the result. Since in the Low case ty_protect Low T2 = T2, this is exact. Similarly in the sum/prod cases no recursive calls on protected types (structural recursion direct). Good.

But wait, in T_App compatibility with κ.ind=Low, the target type is ty_protect Low T2 = T2, so we need E T2 of the apps; exact match. With κ.ind=High, target = ty_protect High T2 with top High/High → V true, so E trivial. So we never need V at a protected type with a non-trivial structure except through the Low case where it's the same type. 

Similarly, in the T_Abs case with κ.ind=Low, the arrow relation's result is E T2 of apps; and the runtime protection protect Low. Fine.

So the V definition:
```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k => ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\ (match k.ind with Low => erase v1 = erase v2 | High => True end)
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.ind with
      | Low => (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = inl w2 k2 /\ V T1 w1 w2) \/
               (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = inr w2 k2 /\ V T2 w1 w2)
      | High => True
      end
  ...
```
where ann k v := security_le (top_security v) k (using the type's top security, i.e., pass k rather than T).

For the arrow:
```
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.ind with
      | Low => forall r u1 u2, locally_closed u1 -> locally_closed u2 -> E T1 u1 u2 ->
                 E T2 (tm_app v1 u1 r) (tm_app v2 u2 r)
      | High => True
      end
  end
with E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2.
```
Mutual fixpoint with V's recursive calls: V T1 w1 w2 (T1 subterm), V T2; E T1, E T2 (subterms); E's call V T (same T). Coq's guard checker should accept this (this is the standard pattern). Let's test early.

Hmm, but note the `ann k v1 /\ ann k v2` conjunction in every case: when T has top security High/High, ann is trivially true, so V is True if the structural part for ind=High is True. For low cases, ann matters.

Wait, in the arrow Low case, V requires E T2 of the apps. Note T2 is the arrow's codomain. But `ty_protect k.ind T2 = T2` in the Low case. Good.

Now, is the `E` in the same mutual block going to cause issues when I want to unfold? We'll write lemmas:
```
Lemma V_unit : forall k v1 v2, V (Ty_Unit k) v1 v2 <-> ...
Lemma V_sum : ...
Lemma V_arrow : forall T1 T2 k v1 v2, V (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k) v1 v2 <->
  ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\ (k.ind=High \/ (forall r u1 u2, lc u1 -> lc u2 -> E T1 u1 u2 -> E T2 (app v1 u1 r) (app v2 u2 r))).
```
These follow by destructing k.ind and simplification (`simpl` or `cbn [V]`). Actually, `V (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k) v1 v2` reduces by simpl to the match on k.ind; destructing k.ind then simpl. We can prove by cases on k's fields.

Now, the final V_erase lemma. Let's write the statement:
```
Lemma V_erase : forall T v1 v2,
  ground T -> all_ind_low T -> V T v1 v2 -> erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.
```
where all_ind_low is defined on T. Since ground excludes arrows, the arrow case of all_ind_low is irrelevant. Let's define:
```
Fixpoint all_ind_low (T : ty) : Prop :=
  (security_of T).(indirect_reader) = Low /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ => all_ind_low T1 /\ all_ind_low T2
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ => True
  end.
```
Proof by induction on T:
- Ty_Unit k: V gives erase equality directly (since k.ind=Low). Need to reduce V with k.ind=Low. Simpl using the hypothesis.
- Ty_Sum T1 T2 k: ground gives ground T1 ∧ ground T2; all_ind_low gives ind Low and all_ind_low T1,T2. V: ann ∧ ann ∧ structural. Structural with ind Low gives either both inl with V T1 or both inr with V T2. Cases: erase (inl w1 k1) = tm_inl (erase w1) public; IH on T1 gives erase w1 = erase w2 → equality. Same for inr.
- Ty_Prod T1 T2 k: similar with pairs: V gives v1 = pair w1 w2 k1, v2 = pair w1' w2' k2 and V T1 w1 w1', V T2 w2 w2'. Erase: pair (erase w1) (erase w2) public = pair (erase w1') (erase w2') public from IHs.
- Ty_Arrow: ground T = False → contradiction.
Good. We need to destruct the structural match properly; using the V_lemmas (V_sum etc.) will help.

And `transparent_all_ind_low`:
```
Lemma transparent_all_ind_low : forall kappa T, transparent_at kappa T -> kappa.(indirect_reader) = Low -> all_ind_low T.
```
Proof by induction on T: 
- all_ind_low T requires (security_of T).ind=Low: from transparent_at's first component security_le (security_of T) kappa, so (security_of T).ind ≤ kappa.ind = Low → = Low (labels).
- components: transparent_at kappa T gives transparent_at kappa Ti; IH with same kappa.ind=Low gives all_ind_low Ti.
- arrow case: all_ind_low arrow = ind low ∧ True; we can still prove all_ind_low for arrow components; but the IH on T1/T2 works. Fine.
We need a lemma: security_le k1 k2 -> k2.ind=Low -> k1.ind=Low (destruct labels).

Now, the final theorem's `transparent T_result` gives transparent_at (security_of T_result) T_result; with security_of T_result.ind=Low → all_ind_low T_result. Good.

Now, let's revisit the E_sub lemma and V_sub. Also E_protect. Let's define E as the mutual fixpoint's component; but after the Fixpoint, E is a Fixpoint function. We can state lemmas about it.

Potential Coq issue: mutual Fixpoint where E's body starts with `forall`, and V's body pattern matches — the guard condition should be fine. Let's test this early with a small file.

Now, E_protect_low's statement and proof:
```
Lemma E_protect_low : forall T t1 t2, E T t1 t2 -> E T (tm_protect Low t1) (tm_protect Low t2).
Proof.
  intros T t1 t2 HE v1 v2 He1 He2.
  apply HE.
  - apply eval_protect_low_inv in He1. exact He1.
  - apply eval_protect_low_inv in He2. exact He2.
Qed.
```
where `eval_protect_low_inv : forall t v, eval (tm_protect Low t) v -> eval t v`. Proof by induction on the eval derivation? Inversion on eval (protect Low t) v gives either E_Protect: eval t v' and eval (protect_value v' Low) v; or E_Abs? No—head is tm_protect, only E_Protect matches. So inversion gives eval t v0 and eval (protect_value v0 Low) v... Hmm, what's my E_Protect constructor? Let me decide: I'll define E_Protect as:
```
| E_Protect : forall t l v, eval t v -> eval (tm_protect l t) (protect_value v l)
```
Then eval (protect Low t) v gives v = protect_value v0 Low and eval t v0. Since protect_value v0 Low = v0 (lemma for all v0? Let's check protect_value on non-value cases: the default branch returns v; on value cases it joins with Low which is identity. So protect_value v Low = v for all v, provable by destructing v; for abs/pair/etc. need protect_security k Low = k. Good). Then v = v0 and eval t v0 = eval t v. Done, inversion suffices; no induction needed!
But wait: is E_Protect's premise `eval t v` enough? If t is a non-value that's stuck, eval doesn't exist. Fine. And the resulting term protect_value v l: is that the right big-step semantics? protect l t steps along with t until t is a value v, then protect_value v l. Yes, that's captured by the single E_Protect constructor (the small-step chain telescopes). Good. And eval_multi for E_Protect: t ⇓ v (multi) and protect l t ⇓ protect_value v l: by induction on the multi of t? Need multi (protect l t) (protect_value v l): prove by induction on the multi t v: each step t→t' gives protect l t → protect l t' (ST_Protect), and at the end, protect l v → protect_value v l (ST_ProtectValue with value v). Need value v from eval_value. So eval_multi needs the multi congruence for protect and the final step. Good.

So E_Protect with the result protect_value v l is clean.

Let's also define `protect_value_value : value v -> value (protect_value v l)`. For values: unit → unit (value); abs: tm_abs T body (protect_security k l) — value requires locally_closed (abs T body κ) which is lc_at 1 body, unchanged. Good. pair: value pair v1 v2 (protect κ l) needs value v1, value v2 — unchanged. inl/inr similar. And for non-values, protect_value v l = v, hmm, but value v premise excludes non-values. Prove by induction on value v.

`erase_protect_value : forall v l, erase_security (protect_value v l) = erase_security v`. By destructing v; all cases erase to the same because erase_security ignores annotations; for pair/inl/inr the recursive structure: erase (pair v1 v2 κ') = pair (erase v1) (erase v2) public; protect changes only κ'. Good.

`protect_value_low : forall v, protect_value v Low = v`. By destructing v; need protect_security k Low = k: destruct k, destruct reader, indirect: join Low Low = Low; join High Low = High (the match `_,_ => High`); so join x Low = x. Provable by destructing the labels. Note protect_security joins BOTH components with Low; join l Low = l for l ∈ {Low, High}: join Low Low = Low; join High Low = High. Yes.

Similarly `ty_protect_low : ty_protect Low T = T`: by destructing T; need protect_security k Low = k. Good.

`security_of_ty_protect_high : security_of (ty_protect High T) = {| reader := High; indirect_reader := High |}`: destruct T; cbn; protect_security k High = join k.reader High / join k.ind High = High/High. Prove join_l_High.

Now, E_protect general:
```
Lemma E_protect : forall T l t1 t2, E T t1 t2 -> E (ty_protect l T) (tm_protect l t1) (tm_protect l t2).
```
Proof: destruct l.
- High: E at (ty_protect High T), whose top security is High/High → V is true for any values; so E holds trivially: intros v1 v2 _ _; apply V_high; apply security_of_ty_protect_high.
- Low: rewrite ty_protect_low; need E T (protect Low t1) (protect Low t2); apply E_protect_low.
Good.

Now, the T_Protect case of the fundamental theorem uses E_protect exactly.

Now, let's check the T_If case in more detail. T_If:
```
has_type Gamma c (Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public) (Ty_Unit public) k)
has_type Gamma yes T
has_type Gamma no T
flows_to k.reader r
⊢ has_type Gamma (tm_if c yes no r) (ty_protect k.ind T)
```
Goal E (ty_protect k.ind T) (if c1 yes1 no1 r) (if c2 yes2 no2 r).
- If k.ind=High: ty_protect High T top = High/High → V_high → E trivial.
- If k.ind=Low: ty_protect Low T = T. Need E T. Take evals to w1,w2. Invert: E_IfL: eval c (tm_inl v k') ∧ value v ∧ flows_to k'.reader r ∧ eval (protect k'.ind yes) w; E_IfR similar.
  IH_c gives E at the sum type. Note the *declared* sum type is Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public)(Ty_Unit public) k; evaluated condition value inl v k' with ann: k' ≤ k. With k.ind=Low → k'.ind=Low. V at the sum type with k.ind=Low: both runs take the same constructor and payloads related at Ty_Unit public.
  Suppose both inl (the inr case similar): then protect k'.ind = Low (both annotations k1',k2' have ind Low? V gives ann: security_le k1' k and security_le k2' k, and k.ind=Low → k1'.ind=Low, k2'.ind=Low). So eval (protect Low yes_i) w_i → eval yes_i w_i. Then IH_yes: E T (subst yes1) (subst yes2) applied to evals gives V T w1 w2. 
  But wait: the eval inversion for E_IfL gives the condition value inl v k' and the branch evaluation eval (protect k'.ind yes) w. The IH_yes is about the branch term yes, and the runtime evaluates protect k'.ind yes. With k'.ind=Low, inversion gives eval yes w. Good.
  
  But hold on: the two runs could have different constructor annotations k1',k2' but both Low-ind. Fine.
  
  What if one run takes inl and the other inr? V at the sum type with ind Low requires matching constructors, so this case is impossible given V. Good.

Now, T_Case similarly, but the branch bodies are under a binder; we handle with env extension.

Let's also think about T_Pair compatibility: T_Pair: has_type t1 T1 -> has_type t2 T2 -> wf_security κ -> has_type (pair t1 t2 κ) (Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ). Goal V at prod of the pair values. V prod: ann κ (pair v1 v2 κ') with κ'=κ: security_le κ κ; structural if κ.ind=Low: exists w's with the pair structure and V T1 v1 v1', V T2 v2 v2'. The pair's evaluated value is tm_pair a1 a2 κ where a_i are the values of t_i; IH1 gives V T1 a1 a1', IH2 gives V T2 a2 a2'. Good. If κ.ind=High: True.

T_Inl: T_Inl: has_type t T1 -> wf_ty T2 -> wf_security κ -> has_type (inl t κ) (Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ). Goal V at sum of the value tm_inl a κ: ann (security_le κ κ); structural if κ.ind=Low: exists w1 w2 k1 k2 with a related at T1: yes with k1=k2=κ, IH gives V T1 a1 a2. If High: True.

T_Inr similar.

T_Fst: T_Fst: has_type t (Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ) -> flows_to κ.reader r -> has_type (fst t r) (ty_protect κ.ind T1). Goal: if κ.ind=High trivial; else E T1 of fst terms. Take evals; invert E_Fst: eval t (tm_pair v1 v2 k) ∧ value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ flows_to k.reader r ∧ eval (protect k.ind v1) w. IH gives E at the prod type; apply to evals: V at prod type with ind Low. Gives ann: security_le k κ → k.ind=Low; and V T1 v1 v1', V T2 v2 v2'. Then eval (protect Low v1) w → eval v1 w (but v1 is a value; we need w = v1? Let's see: eval (protect Low v1) w. Inversion E_Protect: eval v1 v0 ∧ w = protect_value v0 Low. Since v1 is a value, eval v1 v0 gives v0 = v1 by uniqueness. w = protect_value v1 Low = v1. So V T1 w1 w2 = V T1 v1 v1' from the prod relation. Good.

Now, let's consider a subtle issue with subst_env and the identity environment. We'll define `id_env : atom -> tm := fun x => tm_fvar x`. The other cases should be syntactically similar; the recursions: subst_env's clauses must match subst's clauses exactly. Let's define subst_env's Fixpoint mirroring subst exactly with fvar replaced. Yes.

Now, let's also double check the `context_has_type` and the choice of x: context_has_type C T_secret T_result = exists L, forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update empty x T_secret) (plug C (tm_fvar x)) T_result. We choose x := S (max_list (L ++ context_atoms C)). We need ~ In x L (from x > max_list L) and ~ In x (context_atoms C).

Now, what is context_atoms? We need to define it: collect fv of all fixed terms in C. Let's define:
```
Fixpoint ctx_atoms (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs T C' k => ctx_atoms C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t2 r => ctx_atoms C' ++ fv t2
  | C_AppRight t1 C' r => fv t1 ++ ctx_atoms C'
  ...
  end.
```
And prove `subst_plug : forall C x u, ~ In x (ctx_atoms C) -> subst x u (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C u` by induction on C, using subst_fresh for the fixed terms.

Let me make sure the induction works for C_CaseLeft etc.: plug (C_CaseLeft t0 C' body2 r) t = tm_case t0 (plug C' t) body2 r. subst distributes into tm_case's three arguments: subst x u t0, subst x u (plug C' (fvar x)), subst x u body2. Since x ∉ ctx_atoms C = fv t0 ++ ctx_atoms C' ++ fv body2, we have x ∉ fv t0 and x ∉ fv body2 → subst_fresh. IH gives the middle. RHS plug C u = tm_case t0 (plug C' u) body2 r. Good.

Similarly for C_Abs: plug = tm_abs T1 (plug C' t) κ; ctx_atoms (C_Abs T1 C' κ) = ctx_atoms C'. subst into abs = abs (subst x u (plug C' (fvar x))) κ. IH. RHS = abs T1 (plug C' u) κ. Good.

If Hx is false, the theorem is vacuous for that C? No—we have Hx as a hypothesis; if it's contradictory, we can derive anything. Fine. So our proof works regardless.

Wait, but there's a subtlety: we need the env relation for the fundamental theorem: env_rel (update empty x T_secret) σ1 σ2 where σ1 = extend x t1 id, σ2 = extend x t2 id. Note: V references `eval`, so eval must be defined before V. Also `ann` and `top_security`.

Let me write:

```
Definition top_security (v : tm) : security :=
  match v with
  | tm_unit k => k
  | tm_abs _ _ k => k
  | tm_pair _ _ k => k
  | tm_inl _ k => k
  | tm_inr _ k => k
  | _ => public
  end.

Definition ann (k : security) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  security_le (top_security v) k.
```

Then the mutual fixpoint:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k => ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\ match k.(indirect_reader) with Low => erase_security v1 = erase_security v2 | High => True end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security), v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ V T1 w1 w2)
          \/ (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security), v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\ V T2 w1 w2)
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => exists (a1 b1 a2 b2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
          v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\ V T1 a1 a2 /\ V T2 b1 b2
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => forall (r : label) (u1 u2 : tm),
          locally_closed u1 -> locally_closed u2 -> E T1 u1 u2 ->
          E T2 (tm_app v1 u1 r) (tm_app v2 u2 r)
      | High => True
      end
  end
with E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  forall (v1 v2 : tm), eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2.
```

Note: `k.(indirect_reader)` on a variable `k : security`: the projection notation works.

Let's test compile.

As suspected: E calling V with the same argument isn't accepted in a mutual fixpoint. Standard workaround: define V and E where E is inlined in V, or define the mutual fixpoint over a "size" measure, or use `Fixpoint V ... with E ...` where E's recursive call to V is on a subterm — not possible.

Options:
1. Inline E in V's arrow case (write the forall-eval form), and define E separately as a Definition after V. Then V is a plain Fixpoint. In V's arrow case, the argument relation E T1 u1 u2 must be inlined as `forall w1 w2, eval u1 w1 -> eval u2 w2 -> V T1 w1 w2`. That's fine, just verbose.

2. Define E first as an inductive/definition that takes V as a parameter... Type-theoretically, define V with a parameter `Erel : ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop`? Then V is not recursive on E. E.g.:
```
Fixpoint V_gen (Erel : ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) :=
  ... Erel T1 u1 u2 ... Erel T2 (apps)...
```
Then define V T := V_gen E T? Circular again since E = fun T t1 t2 => forall v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2. But with the parameter trick we can use a *fixed-point combinator* at the level of Props? Not directly.

Option 1 is simplest: inline E in V's arrow clause, and define E after V. All lemmas about E then unfold to the forall-eval form. When proving V_arrow's equivalence lemma, the statement can use E (defined after V) and the definitional equality holds (E is a Definition, so `E T1 u1 u2` unfolds to the forall-eval form; `reflexivity`/`split` should work after `cbn`).

Actually, if E is `Definition E T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2`, then `E T1 u1 u2` is definitionally equal to the inlined form. So V_arrow lemma can state it with E and prove by `reflexivity`? The V arrow clause has the inlined form; the lemma states with E which unfolds to the same. Should be proven by `destruct k.ind; simpl; tauto` (or `reflexivity` on Prop equality? Use `split`/`intros`).

Let me restructure:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => forall (r : label) (u1 u2 : tm),
          locally_closed u1 -> locally_closed u2 ->
          (forall (x1 x2 : tm), eval u1 x1 -> eval u2 x2 -> V T1 x1 x2) ->
          (forall (y1 y2 : tm), eval (tm_app v1 u1 r) y1 -> eval (tm_app v2 u2 r) y2 ->
             V T2 y1 y2)
      | High => True
      end
  end.

Definition E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2.
```

The result relation is V T2 y1 y2 (since ty_protect Low T2 = T2). Good.

Labels/security:
```
Lemma flows_to_refl : forall l, flows_to l l.
Proof. destruct l; simpl; auto. Qed.
Lemma flows_to_trans : forall l1 l2 l3, flows_to l1 l2 -> flows_to l2 l3 -> flows_to l1 l3.
Proof. destruct l1,l2,l3; simpl; auto. Qed.
Lemma flows_to_high_low : ~ flows_to High Low. Proof. simpl. auto. Qed.
Lemma not_flows_to : forall l1 l2, ~ flows_to l1 l2 -> l1 = High /\ l2 = Low.
Proof. destruct l1,l2; simpl; tauto. Qed.
Lemma flows_to_high_r : forall l, flows_to High l -> l = High. (destruct l)
Lemma flows_to_l_low : forall l, flows_to l Low -> l = Low. (destruct l)
Lemma join_comm? join_low_l: join l Low = l. join_low_r: join Low l = l? Let's check join Low High = High; join High Low = High; join Low Low=Low. So join Low l = l (destruct l). join l Low = l (destruct l).
Lemma join_high_l : join High l = High; join l High = High (destruct l).
```

Lemma lc_at_mono : forall k k' t, k <= k' -> lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t.

Let me verify: lc_at k (tm_abs T body κ) = lc_at (S k) body; lc_at k' (abs) = lc_at (S k') body, and S k ≤ S k'. So by induction with the same structure, yes. Prove by induction on t generalizing k,k'; lia for bvar; split/conj for compounds.
```
Lemma open_rec_lc : forall k t, lc_at k t -> forall u, open_rec k u t = t.
```
Induction on t generalizing k; bvar case: lc_at k (bvar i) = i<k, open_rec k u (bvar i) = if i=?k then u else bvar i; since i<k, i≠k → unchanged. Use Nat.eqb_neq / Nat.eqb_eq. `apply Nat.eqb_neq; lia`.
Binder case: lc_at k (abs T body κ) = lc_at (S k) body; open_rec k u (abs ...) = abs T (open_rec (S k) u body) κ; IH body (S k) with lc_at (S k) body → open_rec (S k) u body = body. Good.

T_App: lc_at 0 t1 ∧ lc_at 0 t2 → app lc. Etc.

Need `exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L`. Define max_list.

```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with [] => 0 | x :: xs => Nat.max x (max_list xs) end.
Lemma max_list_spec : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Lemma exists_fresh : forall (L : list atom), exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof. intros L; exists (S (max_list L)); intro H; pose proof (max_list_spec _ _ H); lia. Qed.
```

Now the big proof V_sub. Let me think about how to write it conveniently. Since V's definition pattern matches on the type structure, proving V_sub by induction on the subtype derivation requires case analysis on T and U shapes. It might be easier to prove V_sub by *destructing T and U* first (they must match in shape according to the subtype derivation), then induction on the subtype derivation? Hmm, the subtype derivation determines the relation; a generic induction on the derivation with the motive as stated will produce goals where T and U are the constructor forms. Let's write:

```
Lemma V_sub : forall T U, subtype T U -> forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2.
Proof.
  intros T U Hs. induction Hs; intros v1 v2 HV.
  - (* S_Unit *) ...
```
But wait: the induction motive must include the forall v1 v2 V T -> V U part; using `intros T U Hs; induction Hs` gives motive `fun T U Hs => forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2` if we do `induction Hs` after generalizing? Let's do `intros T U Hs. induction Hs.` then the goal for each case is `forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2`? Actually Coq's induction on Hs automatically generalizes the conclusion which mentions T,U,Hs; the motive is inferred as `fun T U (H:subtype T U) => forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2` if we first `intros` the v's? Hmm. The standard way:

```
Lemma V_sub : forall T U, subtype T U -> forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2.
Proof.
  intros T U Hs.
  induction Hs.
```
After `intros T U Hs`, the goal is `forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2`, and `induction Hs` will generalize T, U, and the conclusion (which mentions T,U) to get the motive `fun T U H => forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2`. Hmm, actually `induction Hs` with a goal not depending on Hs... The goal depends on T and U which are arguments of Hs? They are variables introduced by intros, and Hs : subtype T U. Coq's induction will revert the dependencies (T, U) automatically only if the goal depends on them. Yes, induction on Hs will generalize T and U as needed, giving goals for each constructor with the conclusion `forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2` for the constructor's T,U. The IHs will be exactly what we need. Good.

Lemma eval_app_inv : forall t1 t2 r w,

  exists T body k arg,

```

```coq
(* ===== Basic list/freshness helpers ===== *)

Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (max_list xs)
  end.

Lemma max_list_spec : forall (l : list nat) (x : nat), In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y ys IH]; simpl; intros x Hin.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + transitivity (max_list ys); [apply IH; auto | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x : atom, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (max_list L)). intro H.
  pose proof (max_list_spec L _ H). lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh_2 : forall L1 L2 : list atom, exists x : atom, ~ In x L1 /\ ~ In x L2.
Proof.
  intros. destruct (exists_fresh (L1 ++ L2)) as [x Hx].
  exists x. split; intro H; apply Hx; apply in_or_app; auto.
Qed.
```
Actually I can generalize: exists x not in L1 /\ not in L2 directly via max_list (L1++L2) and in_app_or; but `in_app_or` is in List. Use `apply in_app_or`? The direction: In x (L1 ++ L2) -> In x L1 \/ In x L2 is `in_app_or`. Then ~ In x (L1++L2) → ~In x L1 ∧ ~In x L2 by tauto. Good.

  intros v Hv. induction Hv; intros l'; simpl.
  - apply v_unit.
  - apply v_abs; simpl. exact H.
```

```coq
(* ============================================================ *)
(* Part 1: basic label / security / type lemmas                  *)
(* ============================================================ *)

Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (max_list xs)
  end.

Lemma max_list_spec : forall (l : list nat) (x : nat), In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y ys IH]; simpl; intros x Hin.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + transitivity (max_list ys); [apply IH; auto | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x : atom, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (max_list L)). intro H.
  pose proof (max_list_spec L _ H). lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh_two : forall L1 L2 : list atom, exists x : atom, ~ In x L1 /\ ~ In x L2.
Proof.
  intros L1 L2. destruct (exists_fresh (L1 ++ L2)) as [x Hx].
  exists x. split; intro H; apply Hx; apply in_or_app; auto.
Qed.
```

```coq
(* ============================================================ *)
(* Locally nameless infrastructure                               *)
(* ============================================================ *)

Lemma lc_at_mono : forall (k k' : nat) (t : tm), k <= k' -> lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t.
Proof.
  intros k k' t Hle. revert k k' Hle. induction t; simpl; intros k k' Hle H; auto; try lia.
  - (* abs *) apply IHt. lia. exact H.
  - (* app *) destruct H as [H1 H2]; split; [apply IHt1 | apply IHt2]; auto.
  ...
Qed.
```
Hmm, the induction over tm: constructors with subterms: tm_bvar, tm_fvar, tm_unit, tm_abs, tm_app, tm_pair, tm_fst, tm_snd, tm_inl, tm_inr, tm_case, tm_protect, tm_if. Writing a robust tactic: `induction t; simpl; intros k k' Hle H; try lia; try tauto; try (destruct H as [H1 H2]; split; auto); ...`. This is fiddly. Let me think of a uniform approach.

Actually, maybe simpler: prove a more general monotonicity using the equality `lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t` by induction on t with a case analysis done by `intuition`? The structure of lc_at mirrors terms; we can write:

```
Lemma lc_at_mono : forall (k k' : nat) (t : tm), k <= k' -> lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t.
Proof.
  intros k k' t. revert k k'. induction t; simpl; intros k k' Hle H.
  - lia.
  - exact I.
  - exact I.
  - apply IHt. lia. exact H.

Then subst_env definition and lemmas:
```
Fixpoint subst_env (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_unit k => tm_unit k
  | tm_abs T body k => tm_abs T (subst_env sigma body) k
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2) r
  ...
  end.
Definition id_env : atom -> tm := fun x => tm_fvar x.
Definition extend (x : atom) (u : tm) (sigma : atom -> tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y.

Lemma subst_env_extend_id : forall x u t, subst_env (extend x u id_env) t = subst x u t.
```
Proof by induction on t; for fvar y: LHS = extend x u id_env y = if x=?y then u else fvar y; RHS = subst x u (fvar y) = if x=?y then u else fvar y. reflexivity.
Other cases: simpl; rewrite IHs; reflexivity. The binder cases: both use subst_env/subst on body identically. Good.

lc_at_mono:
```
Lemma lc_at_mono : forall (k k' : nat) (t : tm), k <= k' -> lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t.
Proof.
  intros k k' t. revert k k'.
  induction t as [ i | a | s | T body IHbody s0 | t1 IHt1 t2 IHt2 r | t1 IHt1 t2 IHt2 s
                 | t IHt r | t IHt r | t IHt s | t IHt s | t0 IHt0 b1 IHb1 b2 IHb2 r
                 | l t IHt | t0 IHt0 y IHy n IHn r ];
  simpl; intros k k' Hle H.
  - lia.
  - exact I.
  - exact I.
  - apply IHbody; [lia | exact H].
  - destruct H as [H1 H2]; split; [apply IHt1 with (k:=k) | apply IHt2 with (k:=k)]; auto.
  - destruct H as [H1 H2]; split; [apply IHt1 with (k:=k) | apply IHt2 with (k:=k)]; auto.
  - apply IHt with (k:=k); auto.
  - apply IHt with (k:=k); auto.
  - apply IHt with (k:=k); auto.
  - apply IHt with (k:=k); auto.
  - destruct H as [H1 [H2 H3]]; split; [apply IHt0 with (k:=k) | split; [apply IHb1 with (k:=S k) | apply IHb2 with (k:=S k)]]; auto; lia.
  - apply IHt with (k:=k); auto.
  - destruct H as [H1 [H2 H3]]; split; [apply IHt0 with (k:=k) | split; [apply IHy with (k:=k) | apply IHn with (k:=k)]]; auto.
Qed.
```
Note: In the abs case, after simpl, H : lc_at (S k) body, goal lc_at (S k') body; apply IHbody with (k := S k)? `apply IHbody` should unify the goal's `lc_at (S k') body` with IHbody's conclusion `lc_at ?k' body` and the hypothesis conclusion `lc_at ?k body` with H; then leaves `?k <= S k'`; lia with Hle. Let me write `apply IHbody; [lia | exact H]`? The IH is `forall k k', k <= k' -> lc_at k body -> lc_at k' body`. `apply IHbody` with goal `lc_at (S k') body` unifies k'=S k' and body; leaves goals `?k <= S k'` and `lc_at ?k body`. The second matches H : lc_at (S k) body, but Coq applies in order: first `?k <= S k'`, then `lc_at ?k body`. With bullets [lia | exact H], lia sees ?k as an evar: `?k <= S k'` — lia can't solve with an unresolved evar and no info. Hmm. Better: `apply (IHbody (S k) (S k'))`; then goals `S k <= S k'` (lia from Hle) and `lc_at (S k) body` (exact H). Or `eapply IHbody; [lia | exact H]` might unify ?k with S k via the second goal (eassumption). Let's do explicit: `apply (IHbody (S k) (S k')); [lia | exact H]`. Hmm, the argument order of IHbody is (k k' Hle H): `IHbody (S k) (S k')` gives goals S k <= S k' and lc_at (S k) body. Good.

For the app case, `apply IHt1 with (k := k)` gives goal `lc_at k t1 -> lc_at ?k' t1`? Hmm, `apply IHt1 with (k := k)` where IHt1 : forall k k', k <= k' -> lc_at k t1 -> lc_at k' t1 and goal lc_at k' t1: applying with k:=k leaves k' to unify with the goal's k', and goals k <= k' and lc_at k t1. So `apply IHt1 with (k:=k); auto` works: `k <= k'` from Hle, `lc_at k t1` from H1. Good. Alternatively `apply (IHt1 k k'); auto`.

In the case constructor (11), the branch bodies are under a binder: H2 : lc_at (S k) b1, goal lc_at (S k') b1. Use `apply (IHb1 (S k) (S k')); [lia | exact H2]`. The sequencing `auto; lia` means run auto on all goals, then lia on all remaining. Should be fine.

Now open_rec_lc. Use the same induction pattern:
```
Lemma open_rec_lc : forall (k : nat) (t : tm), lc_at k t -> forall u : tm, open_rec k u t = t.
Proof.
  intros k t. revert k. induction t as [...]; simpl; intros k Ht u.

Lemma open_lc_at : forall (k : nat) (u t : tm), lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t) -> lc_at (S k) t.
Proof.
  intros k u t. revert k. induction t as [...]; simpl; intros k Hu Ht.
  - (* bvar i *) destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst i. lia.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. simpl in Ht. lia.
    (* goal lc_at (S k) (bvar i) = i < S k; after destruct, Ht : if ... ; simpl in Ht reduces the if *)
  - fvar/unit: exact I.
  - abs: Ht : lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u body); goal lc_at (S (S k)) body; apply (IHbody (S k)); [apply lc_at_mono with (k:=k); auto; lia | exact Ht].
  - app: destruct Ht as [H1 H2]; split; eapply IHt1; eauto. Need IHt1 : forall k, lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t1) -> lc_at (S k) t1. apply with (k:=k). 
  ...
Qed.
```

- true: Ht : lc_at k u; goal i < S k with k = i → lia.
- false: Ht : lc_at k (tm_bvar i) = i < k; goal i < S k; lia.
Good.

For the abs case: IHbody has the form (after revert k): `forall k, lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u body) -> lc_at (S k) body`. Apply with k := S k: needs lc_at (S k) u (mono) and lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u body) (Ht). Goal lc_at (S (S k)) body. Good.

For app: IHt1 : forall k, lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t1) -> lc_at (S k) t1. Goal lc_at (S k) t1. Apply with k:=k: needs lc_at k u (Hu), lc_at k (open_rec k u t1) (H1). Good.

The IH is named H2 (not IHH). Coq names IHs as H, H0, H1, ... continuing the premise numbering. So in T_Abs: H (wf), H0 (wf), H1 (forall premise), H2 (IH). This is consistent: premises are named H, H0, H1, ... in order, with IHs taking their position.

So `induction H` names all premises sequentially H, H0, H1, ... and IHs are among them. This is workable: for each rule I can refer to the IH by its positional name. Let me derive the names per rule from the induction principle structure:

T_Var: lookup premise (no IH). No IH.
T_Unit: no IH.
T_Abs: H (wf_ty T1), H0 (wf_security kappa), H1 (forall premise), H2 (IH: forall x, ~In x L -> <motive at premise>).
T_App: H (has_type t1 arrow), H0 (IH for t1), H1 (has_type t2), H2 (IH), H3 (flows_to).
T_Pair: H (has_type t1), H0 (IH), H1 (has_type t2), H2 (IH), H3 (wf_security).
T_Fst: H (has_type t), H0 (IH), H1 (flows_to).
T_Snd: same.
T_Inl: H (has_type t), H0 (IH), H1 (wf_ty T2), H2 (wf_security).
T_Inr: H (wf_ty T1), H0 (has_type t), H1 (IH), H2 (wf_security).
T_Case: H (has_type t sum), H0 (IH), H1 (flows_to), H2 (forall b1 premise), H3 (IH b1), H4 (forall b2 premise), H5 (IH b2).
T_Protect: H (has_type t T), H0 (IH).
T_Sub: H (has_type t T), H0 (IH), H1 (subtype T U).
T_If: H (has_type c cond), H0 (IH), H1 (has_type yes), H2 (IH), H3 (has_type no), H4 (IH), H5 (flows_to).

Note the naming is by premise order; but careful: the IH appears immediately after its premise. For T_App: premises: has_type t1 (arrow), then its IH; has_type t2 T1, then its IH; flows_to. So names H (t1), H0 (IH), H1 (t2), H2 (IH), H3 (flows). Yes.

For T_If: H (c), H0 (IH c), H1 (yes), H2 (IH yes), H3 (no), H4 (IH no), H5 (flows). Good.

But caution: this naming pattern holds when the induction is done directly on H with the goal's variables introduced via `intros` — the premises of the constructor get names H, H0, ... including IHs. When the premise is itself a forall (like T_Abs's), it gets one name (H1) and its IH gets H2.

Lemma has_type_fv : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
    forall x : atom, In x (fv t) -> exists U : ty, lookup_context x Gamma = Some U.

  - (* Abs *) intros y Hy. simpl in Hy. (* Hy : In y (fv body) *)
    destruct (exists_fresh_two L [y]) as [x0 [HL Hxy]].
    apply H2 in ...

has_type_lc:
```
Lemma has_type_lc : forall (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty),
    has_type Gamma t T -> locally_closed t.
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T HT.
  induction HT as
    [ Gamma0 x T0 Hlook Hwf
    | Gamma0 k Hwf
    | L Gamma0 T1 body T2 k Hwf1 Hwf2 Hprem IHprem
    | Gamma0 t1 t2 T1 T2 k r H1 IH1 H2 IH2 Hflows
    | Gamma0 t1 t2 T1 T2 k H1 IH1 H2 IH2 Hwf
    | Gamma0 t T1 T2 k r H1 IH1 Hflows
    | Gamma0 t T1 T2 k r H1 IH1 Hflows
    | Gamma0 t T1 T2 k H1 IH1 Hwf2 Hwfsec
    | Gamma0 t T1 T2 k Hwf1 H1 IH1 Hwfsec
    | L Gamma0 t b1 b2 T1 T2 T0 k r H1 IH1 Hflows Hprem1 IHprem1 Hprem2 IHprem2
    | Gamma0 t T0 l H1 IH1
    | Gamma0 t T0 U H1 IH1 Hsub
    | Gamma0 c yes no k T0 r H1 IH1 H2 IH2 H3 IH3 Hflows ].
  - simpl. exact I.
  - simpl. exact I.
  - unfold locally_closed; simpl.
    destruct (exists_fresh L) as [x Hx].

Lemma has_type_fv : forall (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty),
    has_type Gamma t T ->
    forall x : atom, In x (fv t) -> exists U : ty, lookup_context x Gamma = Some U.
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T HT.
  induction HT as [ ... same pattern ... ].
  - (* Var *) intros y Hy. simpl in Hy. destruct Hy as [-> | []].
    exists T0. exact Hlook.
  - (* Unit *) intros y Hy. simpl in Hy. contradiction.
  - (* Abs *) intros y Hy. simpl in Hy.
    destruct (exists_fresh_two L [y]) as [x [HL Hxy]].
    pose proof (IHprem x HL y (fv_open_rec_incl 0 (tm_fvar x) body y Hy)) as [U HU].
    exists U. rewrite lookup_context_update_neq in HU.
    + exact HU.
    + intro E; subst x; apply Hxy; simpl; auto.
  - (* App *) intros y Hy. simpl in Hy. apply in_app_or in Hy as [Hy|Hy]; [apply IH1|apply IH2]; auto.
  - Pair: same.
  - Fst/Snd: apply IH1.
  - Inl: IH1. Inr: IH1.
  - Case: ...
  - Protect: IH1.
  - Sub: IH1.
  - If: split; IH1/IH2/IH3.
Qed.
```

Definitions:

```coq
Definition top_security (v : tm) : security :=
  match v with
  | tm_unit k => k
  | tm_abs _ _ k => k
  | tm_pair _ _ k => k
  | tm_inl _ k => k
  | tm_inr _ k => k
  | _ => public
  end.

Definition ann (k : security) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  security_le (top_security v) k.

Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => erase_security v1 = erase_security v2
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
              v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ V T1 w1 w2)
          \/ (exists (w1 w2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
              v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\ V T2 w1 w2)
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          exists (a1 b1 a2 b2 : tm) (k1 k2 : security),
            v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\ V T1 a1 a2 /\ V T2 b1 b2
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      ann k v1 /\ ann k v2 /\
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => forall (r : label) (u1 u2 : tm),
          locally_closed u1 -> locally_closed u2 ->
          (forall (x1 x2 : tm), eval u1 x1 -> eval u2 x2 -> V T1 x1 x2) ->
          (forall (y1 y2 : tm), eval (tm_app v1 u1 r) y1 -> eval (tm_app v2 u2 r) y2 ->
             V T2 y1 y2)
      | High => True
      end
  end.

Definition E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, eval t1 v1 -> eval t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2.
```
